# Catalonia

Not to be confused with [Catania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catania), [Cataonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cataonia), or [Catatonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catatonia). For the historic states, see [Principality of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Catalonia) and [Catalan Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_Republic). For other uses, see [Catalonia (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalonia_\(disambiguation\)), [Catalunya (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalunya_\(disambiguation\)), and [Cataluña (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalu%C3%B1a_\(disambiguation\)).

**Catalonia**[^cite_note-pronunciation-1] is an [autonomous community of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_community_of_Spain), designated as a *[nationality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalities_and_regions_of_Spain)* by its [Statute of Autonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Autonomy_of_Catalonia_of_2006).[^cite_note-14][^cite_note-15] Its territory is situated on the northeast of the [Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula), to the south of the [Pyrenees](24707.md) mountain range. Catalonia is administratively divided into four [provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provinces_of_Spain) or eight [*vegueries*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vegueria) (regions), which are in turn divided into 43 [*comarques*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comarques_of_Catalonia). The capital and largest city, [Barcelona](4443.md), is the second-most populous [municipality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipalities_in_Spain) in [Spain](26667.md) and the fifth-most populous [urban area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_metropolitan_areas_in_Europe) in the [European Union](9317.md).[^cite_note-demographia-16]

Modern-day Catalonia comprises most of the medieval and early modern [Principality of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Catalonia), with the remainder of the [northern area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Catalonia) now part of France's [Pyrénées-Orientales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyr%C3%A9n%C3%A9es-Orientales). It is bordered by [France](5843419.md) ([Occitanie](45093609.md)) and [Andorra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andorra) to the north, the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md) to the east, and the Spanish autonomous communities of [Aragon](39443.md) to the west and [Valencia](20514168.md) to the south. In addition to its approximately 580 km of coastline, Catalonia also has major high landforms such as the [Pyrenees](24707.md) and the [Pre-Pyrenees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Pyrenees), the Transversal Range (*Serralada Transversal*) or the Central Depression.[^cite_note-17] The official languages are [Catalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_language), [Spanish](26825.md), and the [Aranese dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aranese_dialect) of [Occitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occitan_language).[^cite_note-estatut_langs-18] Additionally, [Catalan Sign language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_Sign_language) is recognized since 2010 upon Law 17/2010.[^cite_note-estatut_langs-18][^cite_note-19]

In 1137, the [County of Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_of_Barcelona)[^cite_note-20] and the [Kingdom of Aragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Aragon) formed a [dynastic union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dynastic_union), resulting in a [composite monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Composite_monarchy), the [Crown of Aragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_of_Aragon). Within the Crown, Barcelona and the other [Catalan counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_counties) merged into a state,[^cite_note-ryder-21] the [Principality of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Catalonia), with its distinct institutional system, such as [Courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Court_of_Catalonia), [Generalitat](294461.md), and [Constitutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutions_of_Catalonia), being the base and promoter for the Crown's Mediterranean trade and expansionism. [Catalan literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_literature) flourished. In 1516, [Charles V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_V,_Holy_Roman_Emperor) became monarch of the crowns of Aragon and [Castile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_of_Castile), retaining both their previous distinct institutions and legislation. Growing tensions led to the [revolt of the Principality of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reapers'_War) (1640–1652), briefly as a [republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_Republic_\(1640%E2%80%931641\)) under French protection. By the [Treaty of the Pyrenees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_the_Pyrenees) (1659), the [northern parts of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Catalonia) were ceded to France. During the [War of the Spanish Succession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Spanish_Succession) (1701–1714), the states of the Crown of Aragon sided against the [Bourbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Bourbon) [Philip V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_V_of_Spain), but following [Barcelona's capitulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Barcelona_\(1713%E2%80%931714\)) ([11 September 1714](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Day_of_Catalonia)) he imposed a unifying administration across Spain via the [Nueva Planta decrees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nueva_Planta_decrees) which suppressed Catalonia's institutions and [legal system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_system), thus ending its separate status. Catalan as a language of government and literature was eclipsed by Spanish.

In the 19th century, [Napoleonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars) and [Carlist Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlist_Wars) affected Catalonia, however, it experienced industrialisation, as well as a [cultural renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaixen%C3%A7a) coupled with incipient [nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_nationalism) and several [workers' movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workers'_movement). The [Second Spanish Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Spanish_Republic) (1931–1939) granted [self-governance to Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Region_of_Catalonia_\(1931%E2%80%931939\)), restoring the [Generalitat](294461.md) as its government. After the [Spanish Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Civil_War) (1936–1939), the [Francoist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francoist_Spain) dictatorship enacted repressive measures, abolishing self-government and banning again the official use of the Catalan language. After a harsh [autarky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autarky), from the late 1950s Catalonia saw [rapid economic growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_miracle), drawing many workers from across Spain and making it a major industrial and touristic hub. During the [Spanish transition to democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_transition_to_democracy) (1975–1982), the Generalitat and [Catalonia's self-government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Autonomy_of_Catalonia_of_1979) were reestablished, remaining one of the most economically dynamic communities in Spain.

In the 2010s until the 2020s, there was growing support for [Catalan independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_independence_movement). On 27 October 2017, the Catalan Parliament unilaterally declared independence following a [referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Catalan_independence_referendum) that was deemed unconstitutional. The Spanish State enforced direct rule by removing the Catalan government and calling [a snap regional election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Catalan_regional_election). The [Spanish Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Supreme_Court) imprisoned seven former Catalan ministers on charges of rebellion and misuse of public funds, while several others—including then-President [Carles Puigdemont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carles_Puigdemont)—fled to other European countries. Those in prison[^cite_note-23] were pardoned in 2021.

## Etymology and pronunciation

The name "Catalonia" ([Medieval Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_Latin_language): *Cathalaunia*), spelled *Cathalonia*, began to be used for the homeland of the [Catalans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalans) (*Cathalanenses*) in the late 11th century and was probably used before as a territorial reference to the group of counties that comprised part of the March of Gothia and the [March of Hispania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marca_Hispanica) under the control of the [Count of Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Count_of_Barcelona) and his relatives.[^cite_note-24] The origin of the name *Catalunya* is subject to diverse interpretations because of a lack of evidence.

One theory suggests that *Catalunya* derives from the name *Gothia* (or *Gauthia*) *Launia* ("Land of the [Goths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goths)"), since the origins of the Catalan counts, lords and people were found in the March of Gothia, known as *Gothia*, whence *Gothland* > *Gothlandia* > *Gothalania* > *Cathalaunia* > *Catalonia* is theoretically derived.[^cite_note-Books.google.com-25][^cite_note-26] During the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages), [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) chroniclers claimed that *Catalania* derives from the local medley of [Goths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goths) with [Alans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alans), initially constituting a *Goth-Alania*.[^cite_note-27]

Other theories suggest:

- *Catalunya* derives from the term "land of castles", having evolved from the term *castlà* or *castlan*, the medieval term for a [castellan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castellan) (a ruler of a [castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castle)).

  [^cite_note-Books.google.com-25]

  [^cite_note-28]

  &#x20;This theory therefore suggests that the names *Catalunya* and *[Castile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castile_\(historical_region\))* have a common root.

- The source is the Celtic *[catalauni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalauni)*, meaning "chiefs of battle", similar to the Celtic given name [\*Katuwalos](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Reconstruction\:Proto-Celtic/Katuwalos);

  [^cite_note-29]

  &#x20;although the area is not known to have been occupied by the [Celtiberians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celtiberians), a Celtic culture was present within the interior of the [Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula) in pre-Roman times.

  [^cite_note-30]

- The [Lacetani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lacetani), an [Iberian tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberians) that lived in the area and whose name, due to the [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Rome) influence, could have evolved by [metathesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metathesis_\(linguistics\)) to *Katelans* and then *Catalans*.

  [^cite_note-31]

- Miguel Vidal, finding serious shortcomings with earlier proposals (such as that an original -*t*- would have, by normal sound laws in the local Romance languages, developed into -*d*-), suggested an Arabic etymology: *_qattāl_* (‏[قَتَّال](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%D9%82%D8%AA%D8%A7%D9%84#Arabic)‎, pl. *_qattālūn_* قَتَّالُون) – meaning "killer" – could have been applied by Muslims to groups of raiders and bandits on the southern border of the [Marca Hispanica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marca_Hispanica).

  [^cite_note-32]

  &#x20;The name, originally derogatory, could have been reappropriated by Christians as an autonym. This is comparable to attested development of the term [Almogavar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almogavars) in nearby areas. In this model, the name *Catalunya* derives from the plural *qattālūn* while the adjective and language name *català* derives from the singular *qattāl*, both with the addition of common Romance suffixes.

  [^cite_note-33]

In English, *Catalonia* is pronounced [/kætəˈloʊniə/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English). The native name, *Catalunya*, is pronounced [\[kətəˈluɲə\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Catalan) in [Central Catalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Catalan), the most widely spoken variety, and [\[kataˈluɲa\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Catalan) in [North-Western Catalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North-Western_Catalan). The [Spanish](26825.md) name is *Cataluña* ([\[kataˈluɲa\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Spanish)), and the [Aranese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aranese) name is *Catalonha* ([\[kataˈluɲa\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Occitan)).

## History

Main article: [History of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Catalonia)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Catalan history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Catalan_history).

### Prehistory

The first known human settlements in what is now Catalonia were at the beginning of the [Middle Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Paleolithic). The oldest known trace of human occupation is a [mandible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandible) found in [Banyoles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banyoles), described as pre-[Neanderthal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neanderthal), that is, some 200,000 years old; other sources suggest it to be only about one third that old.[^cite_note-34] There are important remains from the [Epipalaeolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epipalaeolithic) or [Mesolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesolithic), dated between 8000 and 5000 BC. The most important sites from these eras, all excavated in the region of [Moianès](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moian%C3%A8s), are the Balma del Gai (Epipaleolithic) and the Balma de l'Espluga.[^cite_note-35] The [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) era began in Catalonia around 5000 BC, although the population was slower to develop fixed settlements thanks to the abundance of woods, which allowed the continuation of a fundamentally [hunter-gatherer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherer) culture, for example, La Draga at Banyoles, an "early Neolithic village which dates from the end of the 6th millennium BC".[^cite_note-36]

The [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age) occurred between 1800 and 700 BC. There were some known settlements in the [low Segre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Segre_\(river\)) zone. The Bronze Age coincided with the arrival of the [Indo-Europeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Indo-Europeans) through the [Urnfield Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urnfield_Culture), whose successive waves of migration began around 1200 BC, and they were responsible for the creation of the first proto-urban settlements.[^cite_note-37] Around the middle of the 7th century BC, the [Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age) arrived in Catalonia.

### Pre-Roman and Roman period



[Aqüeducte de les Ferreres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Ferreres_Aqueduct), [Roman aqueduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_aqueduct) in [Tarragona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarragona)&#x20;

In pre-Roman times, the area that is now Catalonia was populated by the [Iberians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberians). The Iberians tribes – the [Ilergetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilergetes), [Indigetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigetes) and [Lacetani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lacetani) (Cerretains) – also maintained relations with the peoples of the Mediterranean. Some urban agglomerations became relevant, including Ilerda ([Lleida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lleida)) inland, Hibera (perhaps [Amposta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amposta) or [Tortosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tortosa)) or Indika ([Ullastret](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ullastret)). Coastal trading colonies were established by the [ancient Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece), who settled around the [Gulf of Roses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Roses), in Emporion ([Empúries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emp%C3%BAries)) and [Roses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roses,_Girona) in the [8th century BC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonies_in_antiquity).

After the Carthaginian defeat by the [Roman Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Republic), the north-east of Iberia became the first to come under Roman rule and became part of [Hispania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispania), the westernmost part of the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire). [Tarraco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarraco_\(Tarragona\)) (modern [Tarragona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarragona)) was one of the most important Roman cities in Hispania and the capital of the [province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_province) of [Tarraconensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarraconensis). Other important cities of the Roman period are Ilerda (Lleida), Dertosa (Tortosa), Gerunda (Girona) as well as the ports of Empuriæ (former Emporion) and Barcino ([Barcelona](4443.md)). As for the rest of Hispania, [Latin law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_law) was granted to all cities under the reign of [Vespasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vespasian) (69–79 AD), while [Roman citizenship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_citizenship) was granted to all free men of the empire by the [Edict of Caracalla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Caracalla) in 212 AD (Tarraco, the capital, was already a colony of [Roman law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_law) since 45 BC). It was a rich agricultural province (olive oil, wine, [wheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheat)), and the first centuries of the Empire saw the construction of roads (the most important being the [Via Augusta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Via_Augusta), parallel to Mediterranean coastline) and infrastructure like [aqueducts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aqueduct_\(water_supply\)).

Conversion to [Christianity](5211.md), attested in the 3rd century, was completed in urban areas in the 4th century. Although Hispania remained under Roman rule and did not fall under the rule of [Vandals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vandals), [Suebi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suebi) and [Alans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alans) in the 5th century, the main cities suffered frequent sacking and some [deurbanisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deurbanization).

### Middle Ages

&#x20;After the fall of the [Western Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Roman_Empire), the area was conquered by the [Visigoths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visigoths) and was ruled as part of the [Visigothic Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visigothic_Kingdom) for almost two and a half centuries. In 718, it came under [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) control and became part of [Al-Andalus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Andalus), a province of the [Umayyad Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate). From the conquest of Roussillon in 760, to the [conquest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Barcelona_\(801\)) of Barcelona in 801, the [Frankish empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankish_empire) took control of the area between Septimania and the [Llobregat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Llobregat) river from the Muslims and created heavily militarised, self-governing [counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County). These counties formed part of the historiographically known as the [Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Septimania) and [Hispanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_March) Marches, a [buffer zone](489870.md) in the south of the Frankish Empire in the northeast of the Iberian Peninsula, to act as a defensive barrier against further invasions from Al-Andalus.[^cite_note-38]&#x20;



[Ramon Berenguer IV, Count of Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramon_Berenguer_IV,_Count_of_Barcelona) (left), [Petronilla of Aragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petronilla_of_Aragon) (right) and their son Alfonso II of Aragon and I of Barcelona (bottom), dynastic union of the [Crown of Aragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_of_Aragon)

&#x20;These counties came under the rule of the [counts of Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counts_of_Barcelona), who were Frankish [vassals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vassal) nominated by the emperor of the Franks, to whom they were [feudatories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feudatory) (801–988). At the end of the 9th century, the Count of Barcelona [Wilfred the Hairy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilfred_the_Hairy) (878–897) made his titles hereditary and thus founded the dynasty of the [House of Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Barcelona), which reigned in Catalonia until 1410.



Ponç Hug IV, Count of Empúries, and Pero Maça during the conquest of Mallorca (1229)



A 15th-century miniature of the [General Court of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Court_of_Catalonia)

In 988 [Borrell II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borrell_II), Count of Barcelona, did not recognise the new French king [Hugh Capet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugh_Capet) as his king, evidencing the loss of dependency from Frankish rule and confirming his successors (from [Ramon Borrell I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramon_Borrell,_Count_of_Barcelona) onwards) as independent of the Capetian crown.[^cite_note-39] At the beginning of eleventh century the Catalan counties experienced an important process of feudalisation, however, the efforts of church's sponsored [Peace and Truce Assemblies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_and_Truce_of_God) and the intervention of [Ramon Berenguer I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramon_Berenguer_I), count of Barcelona (1035–1076) in the negotiations with the rebel nobility resulted in the partial restoration of the comital authority under the new feudal order. To fulfill that purpose, Ramon Berenguer began the modification of the legislation in the written [Usages of Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usages_of_Barcelona), being one of the first European compilations of feudal law. The earliest known use of the name "[Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Catalonia)" for these counties dates to 1117.

In 1137, [Ramon Berenguer IV, Count of Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramon_Berenguer_IV,_Count_of_Barcelona) decided to accept [King Ramiro II of Aragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramiro_II_of_Aragon)'s proposal to receive the [Kingdom of Aragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Aragon) and to marry his daughter [Petronila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petronilla_of_Aragon), establishing the dynastic union of the County of Barcelona with Aragon, creating a composite monarchy later known as the [Crown of Aragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_of_Aragon) and making the Catalan counties that were vassalized or merged with the County of Barcelona into a [principality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality) of the Aragonese Crown. During the reign of his son [Alphons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfonso_II_of_Aragon), in 1173, Catalonia was regarded as a legal entity for the first time, while the Usages of Barcelona were compiled in the process to turn them into the law and custom of Catalonia (*Consuetudinem Cathalonie*), being considered one of the "milestones of [Catalan political identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_political_Catalanism)".[^cite_note-40] In 1258, by means of the [Treaty of Corbeil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Corbeil_\(1258\)) [James I of Aragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_I_of_Aragon) renounced his family rights and dominions in [Occitania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occitania), while the king of France, [Louis IX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_IX_of_France), formally relinquished to any historical claim of feudal lordship he might have over the Catalan counties.[^cite_note-Petit-Dutaillis2013-41] This treaty confirmed, from French point of view, the independence of the Catalan counties already established the previous three centuries.

As a coastal land, Catalonia became the base of the Aragonese Crown's maritime forces, which spread the power of the Crown in the Mediterranean, turning Barcelona into a powerful and wealthy city. In the period of 1164–1410, new territories, the [Kingdom of Valencia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Valencia), the [Kingdom of Majorca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Majorca), the [Kingdom of Sardinia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Sardinia_\(1324%E2%80%931720\)), the [Kingdom of Sicily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Sicily), and, briefly, the [Duchies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchy) of [Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchy_of_Athens) and [Neopatras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchy_of_Neopatras), were incorporated into the dynastic domains of the [House of Aragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Barcelona). The expansion was accompanied by a great development of the Catalan trade, creating an extensive trade network across the Mediterranean which competed with those of the maritime republics of [Genoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genoa_Republic) and [Venice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Venice).

At the same time, the [Principality of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Catalonia) developed a complex institutional and political system based on the concept of a pact between the [estates of the realm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estates_of_the_realm) and the king. The legislation had to be passed by the [Catalan Courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Court_of_Catalonia) (*Corts Catalanes*), one of the first parliamentary bodies of Europe that, after 1283, officially obtained the power to pass legislation with the monarch.[^cite_note-42] The Courts were composed of the three estates organized into "arms" (*braços*), were presided over by the monarch, and approved the [Constitutions of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutions_of_Catalonia), which established a compilation of rights for the inhabitants of the Principality. In order to collect general taxes, the Catalan Courts of 1359 established a permanent representative body, known as the [Generalitat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generalitat_of_Catalonia), which gained considerable political power over the next centuries.[^cite_note-43]



Diachronic map of the [Crown of Aragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_of_Aragon). The Principality of Catalonia appears in light green.

The domains of the Aragonese Crown were severely affected by the [Black Death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Death) pandemic and by later outbreaks of the [plague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plague_\(disease\)). Between 1347 and 1497 Catalonia lost 37 percent of its population.[^cite_note-44] In 1410, the last reigning monarch of the House of Barcelona, King [Martin I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_of_Aragon) died without surviving descendants. Under the [Compromise of Caspe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compromise_of_Caspe) (1412), the representatives of the kingdoms of Aragon, Valencia and the Principality of Catalonia appointed [Ferdinand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_I_of_Aragon) from the Castilian [House of Trastámara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Trast%C3%A1mara) as King of the Crown of Aragon.[^cite_note-Reilly-45] During the reign of his son, [John II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_II_of_Aragon_and_Navarre), the persistent economic crisis and social and political tensions in the Principality led to the [Catalan Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_Civil_War) (1462–1472) and the [War of the Remences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Remences) (1462–1486) that left Catalonia exhausted. The [Sentencia Arbitral de Guadalupe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sentencia_Arbitral_de_Guadalupe) (1486) liberated the [remença](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remensa) peasants from the feudal "[evil customs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evil_customs)".

In the later Middle Ages, [Catalan literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_literature) flourished in Catalonia proper and in the kingdoms of Majorca and Valencia, with such remarkable authors as the philosopher [Ramon Llull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramon_Llull), the Valencian poet [Ausiàs March](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ausi%C3%A0s_March), and [Joanot Martorell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joanot_Martorell), author of the novel *[Tirant lo Blanch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tirant_lo_Blanch)*, published in 1490.

### Modern era



The [Principality of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Catalonia) (1608 or 1612)

[Ferdinand II of Aragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_II_of_Aragon), the son of John II, and queen [Isabella I of Castile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isabella_I_of_Castile) were married in 1469, later taking the title the [Catholic Monarchs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Monarchs); subsequently, this event was traditionally seen as the first step towards a unified Spain. At this time, though united by marriage, the Crown of [Castile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_of_Castile) and the states of the Crown of Aragon remained distinct polities, each keeping its own institutions, parliaments, laws, jurisdictions, and currency.[^cite_note-46] Castile commissioned expeditions to the [Americas](29833.md) and benefited from the riches acquired in the [colonisation of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_colonization_of_the_Americas), but, in time, also carried the main burden of military expenses of the united Spanish kingdoms. After Isabella's death, Ferdinand II personally ruled both crowns. By virtue of descent from his maternal grandparents, Ferdinand and Isabella, in 1516 [Charles I of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_I_of_Spain) became the first king to rule the Crowns of Castile and Aragon simultaneously by his own right. Following the death of his paternal ([House of Habsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Habsburg)) grandfather, [Maximilian I, Holy Roman Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximilian_I,_Holy_Roman_Emperor), he was also elected [Charles V, Holy Roman Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_V,_Holy_Roman_Emperor), in 1519.[^cite_note-47]



*[Corpus de Sang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corpus_de_Sang)* (7 June 1640), one of the main events of the Reaper's War. Painted in 1910

Over the next few centuries, the Principality of Catalonia was generally on the losing side of a series of wars that led steadily to an increased centralisation of power in Spain. However, between the 16th and 18th centuries, the participation of the political community in the local and the general Catalan government grew (thus consolidating its constitutional system), while the kings remained absent, represented by a [viceroy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_viceroys_of_Catalonia). Tensions between Catalan institutions and the monarchy began to arise. The large and burdensome presence of the Spanish royal army in the Principality due to the [Franco-Spanish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-Spanish_War_\(1635%E2%80%931659\)) led to an uprising of peasants, provoking the [Reapers' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reapers'_War) (1640–1652), which saw Catalonia rebel, briefly as a [republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_Republic_\(1640%E2%80%931641\)) led by the president of the Generalitat, [Pau Claris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pau_Claris) with French help against the Spanish Crown for overstepping Catalonia's rights during the [Thirty Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirty_Years'_War).[^cite_note-48] Within a brief period France took full control of Catalonia. Most of Catalonia was reconquered by the Spanish monarchy but Catalan rights were mostly recognised. [Roussillon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roussillon) and half of Cerdanya was lost to France by the [Treaty of the Pyrenees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_the_Pyrenees) (1659).[^cite_note-pyrconditions-49]

The most significant conflict concerning the governing monarchy was the [War of the Spanish Succession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Spanish_Succession) (1701–1715), which began when the childless [Charles II of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_II_of_Spain), the last Spanish Habsburg, died without an heir in 1700. Charles II had chosen [Philip V of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_V_of_Spain) from the French [House of Bourbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Bourbon). The Principality of Catalonia, like the other states of the Crown of Aragon, rose up in support of the Austrian Habsburg pretender [Charles VI, Holy Roman Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_VI,_Holy_Roman_Emperor), in his claim for the Spanish throne as Charles III of Spain. The fight between the houses of Bourbon and Habsburg for the Spanish Crown split Spain and Europe. After a series of advances and stalemates between the two sides, geopolitical changes in Europe led to peace. Philip V was internationally recognised as king of Spain by the [Treaty of Utrecht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Utrecht) (1713).

In July 1713 the Junta de Braços (parliamentary assembly) of Catalonia opted to unilaterally remain in the war to protect Catalan constitutions and lives from Bourbon punishment, beginning a separate conflict known as the [War of the Catalans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Catalans) (1713–1714). The [siege and capitulation of Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Barcelona_\(1713%E2%80%931714\)) (11 September 1714) ended Catalan resistance. In retaliation for the betrayal, and inspired by the French model, Philip enacted the [Nueva Planta decrees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nueva_Planta_decrees) (1707, 1715 and 1716), forcibly incorporating the realms of the Crown of Aragon, including the Principality of Catalonia in 1716, as provinces of Castile, terminating their status as separate states along with their parliaments, institutions and [public laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_law), within a French-style centralised and [absolutist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_monarchy) kingdom of Spain.[^cite_note-50] After the War of the Spanish Succession, the assimilation of the Crown of Aragon in the Castilian Crown through the Nueva Planta Decrees was the first step in the creation of the Spanish [nation state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation_state).[^cite_note-51] These nationalist policies, sometimes aggressive,[^cite_note-52][^cite_note-53][^cite_note-54][^cite_note-\:03-55] and still in force,[^cite_note-56][^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58] have been and are the seed of repeated territorial conflicts within the state, although, a survey of language usage in 1807, commissioned by [Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon), indicates that, except in the royal courts, Spanish was absent from everyday life.[^cite_note-59] In the second half of the 17th century and the 18th century (excluding the parentheses of the Succession War and the post-war instability) Catalonia carried out a successful process of economic growth and [proto-industrialisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-industrialization), reinforced in the late quarter of the century when Castile's trade monopoly with American colonies ended.

### Late modern history



[Third siege of Girona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_siege_of_Girona) (1809), [Peninsular War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peninsular_War) against Napoleon

At the beginning of the nineteenth century, Catalonia was severely affected by the [Napoleonic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars). In 1808, it was occupied by French troops; the resistance against the occupation eventually developed into the [Peninsular War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peninsular_War). The rejection of French dominion was institutionalised with the creation of "juntas" (councils) who, remaining loyal to the Bourbons, exercised the sovereignty and representation of the territory due to the disappearance of the old institutions. In 1810, Napoleon took direct control of Catalonia, creating the Government of Catalonia under the rule of [Marshall Augereau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles-Pierre_Augereau), and making Catalan briefly an official language again. Between 1812 and 1814, Catalonia was annexed to France.[^cite_note-60] The French troops evacuated Catalan territory at the end of 1814. After the Bourbon restoration in Spain and the death of the absolutist king [Ferdinand VII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_VII) (1833), [Carlist Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlist_Wars) erupted against the newly established [liberal state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism) of [Isabella II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isabella_II). Catalonia was divided, with the coastal and most industrialised areas supporting liberalism, while most of the countryside were in the hands of the [Carlist faction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlism); the latter proposed to reestablish the institutional systems suppressed by the Nueva Planta decrees in the ancient realms of the Crown of Aragon. The consolidation of the liberal state saw a new provincial division of Spain, including Catalonia, which was divided into four provinces (Barcelona, Girona, Lleida and Tarragona).



Suspects rounded up by the [Civil Guard](325008.md) during the [Tragic Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tragic_Week_\(Spain\)), 1909

In the second third of the 19th century, Catalonia became an important industrial center, particularly focused on [textiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_cotton_industry_in_Catalonia). This process was a consequence of the conditions of proto-industrialisation of textile production in the prior two centuries, growing capital from wine and brandy export,[^cite_note-61]<sup>: 27 </sup> and was later boosted by the government support for domestic manufacturing. In 1832, the [Bonaplata Factory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonaplata_Factory) in Barcelona became the first factory in the country to make use of the [steam engine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steam_engine). [^cite_note-62]<sup>: 308 </sup> The first railway on the Iberian Peninsula was built between Barcelona and [Mataró](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matar%C3%B3) in 1848.[^cite_note-63] A policy to encourage [company towns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_town#Industrial_colonies_in_Catalonia) also saw the textile industry flourish in the countryside in the 1860s and 1870s. Although the policy of Spanish governments oscillated between free trade and protectionism, [protectionist laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionist_laws?action=edit\&redlink=1) become more common. To this day Catalonia remains one of the most industrialised areas of Spain.

During the 19th century, as well as other Spaniards, there were Catalan business involved in the [Atlantic slave trade](49005.md) and in the owning and management of [slave plantations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_plantation) in Cuba and Puerto Rico.[^cite_note-64] Between 1817 and 1867, they were involved in the transportation of 550,000 to 700,000 slaves from West Africa to the Caribbean. The Spanish government turned a blind eye to illicit slave trading. When slavery was abolished in 1886, various other Spaniards, the "*[indianos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiano)*", as well as Catalans returned and invested their fortunes in constructing mansions in areas such as [La Rambla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Rambla,_Barcelona).[^cite_note-65]

At the same time, Barcelona was the focus of industrial conflict and revolutionary uprisings known as "bullangues". In Catalonia, a [republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republicanism) trend began to develop among the progressives, attracting many Catalans who favored the federalisation of Spain. Meanwhile, the Catalan language saw a [Romantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism) cultural renaissance from the second third of the century onwards, the *[Renaixença](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaixen%C3%A7a)*, among both the working class and the bourgeoisie. Right after the fall of the [First Spanish Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Spanish_Republic) (1873–1874) and the subsequent restoration of the Bourbon dynasty (1874), Catalan nationalism began to be organised politically under the leadership of the republican federalist [Valentí Almirall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valent%C3%AD_Almirall_i_Llozer).



[Francesc Macià](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francesc_Maci%C3%A0) proclaiming the [Catalan Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_Republic_\(1931\)) on 14 April 1931 in Barcelona

The [anarchist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anarchist) movement had been active throughout the last quarter of the 19th century and the early 20th century, founding the [CNT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederaci%C3%B3n_Nacional_del_Trabajo) trade union in 1910 and achieving one of the first [eight-hour workdays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eight-hour_day) in Europe in 1919.[^cite_note-66] Growing resentment of conscription and of the military culminated in the [Tragic Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tragic_Week_\(Catalonia\)) (Catalan: *Setmana Tràgica*) in Barcelona in 1909. Under the hegemony of the [Regionalist League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regionalist_League_of_Catalonia), Catalonia gained a degree of administrative unity for the first time in the Modern era. In 1914, the four Catalan provinces were authorised to create a [commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Catalonia) (Catalan: *Mancomunitat*), lacking legislative power or political autonomy, which carried out an ambitious program of modernisation, but it was disbanded in 1925 by the dictatorship of [Primo de Rivera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_Primo_de_Rivera) (1923–1930). During the final stage of the Dictatorship, with Spain beginning to suffer an economic crisis, Barcelona hosted the [1929 International Exposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1929_Barcelona_International_Exposition).[^cite_note-MonclúsFraga2006-67]

After the end of the dictatorship and a brief proclamation of the [Catalan Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_Republic_\(1931\)), during the events of the proclamation of the [Second Spanish Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Spanish_Republic) (14–17 April 1931),[^cite_note-68] Catalonia received, in 1932, its [first Statute of Autonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Autonomy_of_Catalonia_of_1932) from the Spanish Republic's Parliament, granting it a considerable degree of self-governance, establishing an autonomous body, the Generalitat of Catalonia, which included a [parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Catalonia). The left-wing pro-independence leader [Francesc Macià](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francesc_Maci%C3%A0) was appointed its first president. Under the Statute, Catalan became an official language. The governments of the [Republican Generalitat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Region_of_Catalonia_\(1931%E2%80%931939\)), led by the [Republican Left of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Left_of_Catalonia) (ERC) leaders Francesc Macià (1931–1933) and [Lluís Companys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Llu%C3%ADs_Companys) (1933–1940), sought to implement a modernizing and progressive social agenda, despite the internal difficulties. This period was marked by political unrest, the effects of the economic crisis and their social repercussions. The Statute of Autonomy was suspended in 1934, due to the [Events of 6 October](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Events_of_6_October) in Barcelona, after the accession of right-wing Spanish nationalist party [CEDA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CEDA) to the government of the Republic, considered close to [fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism).[^cite_note-69] After the electoral victory of the left wing [Popular Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_Front_\(Spain\)) in February 1936, the Government of Catalonia was pardoned and the self-government was restored.

### Spanish Civil War (1936–1939) and Franco's rule (1939–1975)

Main articles: [Revolutionary Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Catalonia) and [Francoist Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francoist_Catalonia)





Left: Anarchist militia during the [Revolution of 1936](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Revolution_of_1936). Right: [Bombing of Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombing_of_Barcelona) (1938)

The defeat of the [military rebellion against the Republican government in Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_1936_military_uprising_in_Barcelona) placed Catalonia firmly in the [Republican side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_faction_\(Spanish_Civil_War\)) of the [Spanish Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Civil_War). During the war, there were two rival powers in Catalonia: the de jure power of the Generalitat and the de facto power of the armed popular militias.[^cite_note-70] Violent confrontations between the workers' parties ([CNT-FAI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CNT-FAI) and [POUM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workers_Party_of_Marxist_Unification) against the [PSUC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unified_Socialist_Party_of_Catalonia)) culminated in the defeat of the former in 1937. The situation resolved itself progressively in favor of the Generalitat, but at the same time the Generalitat lost most of its autonomous powers within Republican Spain. In 1938 Franco's troops broke the Republican territory in two, isolating Catalonia from the rest of the Republican territory. The defeat of the Republican army in the [Battle of the Ebro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Ebro) led in 1938 and 1939 to the [occupation of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalonia_Offensive) by Franco's forces.



CNT-FAI worker [cooperative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooperative) in Barcelona producing wood and steel products

The defeat of the Spanish Republic in the [Spanish Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Civil_War) brought to power the dictatorship of [Francisco Franco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Franco), whose first ten-year rule was particularly violent, autocratic, and repressive both in a political, cultural, social, and economical sense.[^cite_note-71] In Catalonia, any kind of public activities associated with [Catalan nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_nationalism), [republicanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republicanism), [anarchism](12.md), [socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism), [liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism), [democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy) or [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism), including the publication of books on those subjects or simply discussion of them in open meetings, was banned. Franco's regime banned the use of Catalan in government-run institutions and during public events, and the Catalan institutions of self-government were abolished. The president of Catalonia, [Lluís Companys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Llu%C3%ADs_Companys), was taken to Spain from his exile in the German-occupied France and was tortured and executed in the [Montjuïc Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montju%C3%AFc_Castle_\(Barcelona\)) of Barcelona for the crime of 'military rebellion'.[^cite_note-72]

During later stages of [Francoist Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francoist_Spain), certain folkloric and religious celebrations in Catalan resumed and were tolerated. Use of Catalan in the [mass media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media) had been forbidden but was permitted from the early 1950s[^cite_note-73] in the theatre. Despite the ban during the first years and the difficulties of the next period, publishing in Catalan continued throughout his rule.[^cite_note-74]

The years after the war were extremely hard. Catalonia, like many other parts of Spain, had been devastated by the war. Recovery from the war damage was slow and made more difficult by the international trade embargo and the [autarkic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autarky) politics of Franco's regime. By the late 1950s, the region had recovered its pre-war economic levels and in the 1960s was the second-fastest growing economy in the world in what became known as the [Spanish miracle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_miracle). During this period there was a spectacular[^cite_note-75] growth of industry and tourism in Catalonia that drew large numbers of workers to the region from across Spain and made the area around Barcelona one of Europe's largest industrial metropolitan areas.

### Transition and democratic period (1975–present)



The Olympic flame in the [Olympic Stadium Lluís Companys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estadi_Ol%C3%ADmpic_Llu%C3%ADs_Companys) of [Barcelona](4443.md) during the [1992 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Summer_Olympics)

After Franco's death in 1975, Catalonia voted for the adoption of a democratic Spanish Constitution in 1978, in which Catalonia recovered political and cultural autonomy, restoring the Generalitat (exiled since the end of the Civil War in 1939) in 1977 and adopting a [new Statute of Autonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Autonomy_of_Catalonia_of_1979) in 1979, which defined Catalonia as a "nationality". The [first elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Catalan_regional_election) to the Parliament of Catalonia under this Statute gave the Catalan presidency to [Jordi Pujol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordi_Pujol), leader of [Convergència i Unió](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convergence_and_Unity) (CiU), a center-right Catalan nationalist electoral coalition, with Pujol re-elected until 2003. Throughout the 1980s and 1990s, the institutions of Catalan autonomy were deployed, among them an autonomous police force, the [Mossos d'Esquadra](294120.md), in 1983,[^cite_note-76] and the broadcasting network [Televisió de Catalunya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Televisi%C3%B3_de_Catalunya) and its first channel [TV3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TV3_\(Catalonia\)), created in 1983.[^cite_note-77] An extensive program of normalisation of Catalan language was carried out. Today, Catalonia remains one of the most economically dynamic communities of Spain. The Catalan capital and largest city, Barcelona, is a major international cultural centre and a major tourist destination. In 1992, [Barcelona hosted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Summer_Olympics) the [Summer Olympic Games](27566.md).[^cite_note-78]

#### Independence movement

Main articles: [Catalan independence movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_independence_movement) and [2017–18 Spanish constitutional crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017%E2%80%9318_Spanish_constitutional_crisis)

In November 2003, elections to the Parliament of Catalonia gave the government to a left-wing Catalanist coalition formed by the [Socialists' Party of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialists'_Party_of_Catalonia) (PSC-PSOE), [Republican Left of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Left_of_Catalonia) (ERC) and [Initiative for Catalonia Greens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Initiative_for_Catalonia_Greens) (ICV), and the socialist [Pasqual Maragall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasqual_Maragall) was appointed president. The new government prepared a bill for a [new Statute of Autonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Autonomy_of_Catalonia), aiming to consolidate and to expand self-government.

The new Statute of Autonomy of Catalonia, approved after a [referendum in 2006](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Catalan_Statute_of_Autonomy_referendum), was contested by important sectors of Spanish society, especially by the conservative [People's Party](404727.md), which sent the law to the [Constitutional Court of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Spain). In 2010, [the Court ruled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Constitutional_Court_Ruling_on_the_2006_Statute_of_Autonomy_of_Catalonia) non-valid various articles establishing an autonomous Catalan system of Justice, improved financing, a new territorial division, the protected status of Catalan language, the recovering of [historical rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutions_of_Catalonia) via Constitution's First Additional Provision or the symbolical declaration of Catalonia as a nation.[^cite_note-79] This decision was severely contested by large sectors of Catalan society, which increased the demands of independence.[^cite_note-80]



[Catalan president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_Generalitat_of_Catalonia), [Carles Puigdemont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carles_Puigdemont), addresses the crowd following the unilateral declaration of independence on 27 October.

A controversial [independence referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Catalan_independence_referendum) was held in Catalonia on 1 October 2017, using a disputed voting process.[^cite_note-ElPais03102017-81][^cite_note-82] It was declared illegal and suspended by the [Constitutional Court of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Spain), because it breached the [1978 Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Constitution_of_1978).[^cite_note-boe2162017-83][^cite_note-84] Subsequent developments saw, on 27 October 2017, a symbolic [declaration of independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_declaration_of_independence) by the Parliament of Catalonia, the enforcement of [direct rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_rule_over_Catalonia) by the Spanish government through the use of Article 155 of the Constitution,[^cite_note-85][^cite_note-86][^cite_note-87][^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89] the dismissal of the [Executive Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Council_of_Catalonia) and the dissolution of the Parliament, with a [snap regional election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Catalan_regional_election) called for 21 December 2017, which ended with a victory of pro-independence parties.[^cite_note-eldiario.es-27Oct17-90] Former President [Carles Puigdemont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carles_Puigdemont) and five former cabinet ministers fled Spain and took refuge in other European countries (such as [Belgium](3343.md), in Puigdemont's case), whereas nine other cabinet members, including vice-president [Oriol Junqueras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriol_Junqueras), were sentenced to prison under various charges of rebellion, sedition, and misuse of public funds.[^cite_note-91][^cite_note-92] [Quim Torra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quim_Torra) became the [131st](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Presidents_of_the_Government_of_Catalonia) [President of the Government of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_Government_of_Catalonia) on 17 May 2018,[^cite_note-GeneralitatdeCataluny17052018-93] after the Spanish courts blocked three other candidates.[^cite_note-94]

In 2018, the [Assemblea Nacional Catalana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assemblea_Nacional_Catalana) joined the [Unrepresented Nations and Peoples Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unrepresented_Nations_and_Peoples_Organization) (UNPO) on behalf of Catalonia.[^cite_note-95]

On 14 October 2019, the Spanish Supreme court [sentenced several Catalan political leaders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trial_of_Catalonia_independence_leaders), involved in organizing a referendum on Catalonia's independence from Spain, and convicted them on charges ranging from [sedition](324091.md) to [misuse of public funds](209411.md), with sentences ranging from 9 to 13 years in prison. This decision sparked demonstrations around Catalonia.[^cite_note-96] They were later pardoned by the Spanish government and left prison in June 2021.[^cite_note-97][^cite_note-98] In the early-to-mid 2020s support for independence declined to 40% in favour, with support for dropping significantly,[^cite_note-99][^cite_note-100][^cite_note-101][^cite_note-102][^cite_note-103] with the left-wing [unionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_unionism) [Socialists' Party of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialists'_Party_of_Catalonia) (PSC-PSOE) returning to office in the [May 2024 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Catalan_regional_election).[^cite_note-104][^cite_note-105]

## Geography

### Climate



Climates of Catalonia:&#x20;

- &#x20; [Oceanic climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate)

- &#x20; [Alpine climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpine_climate)

- &#x20; Mediterranean climate of alpine influence

- &#x20; Inland Mediterranean climate

- &#x20; [Coastal Mediterranean climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_climate)

- &#x20; Mediterranean climate of [continental influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_climate)

The climate of Catalonia is diverse. The populated areas lying by the coast in Tarragona, Barcelona and Girona provinces feature a [Hot-summer Mediterranean climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot-summer_Mediterranean_climate) ([Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) *Csa*). The inland part (including the Lleida province and the inner part of Barcelona province) show a mostly [Mediterranean climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_climate) ([Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) *Csa*). The Pyrenean peaks have a [continental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_climate) ([Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) *D*) or even [Alpine climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpine_climate) ([Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) *ET*) at the highest summits, while the valleys have a maritime or [oceanic climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_climate) sub-type ([Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) *Cfb*). A [cold semi-arid climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-arid_climate) (Köppen *BSk*) is also present in parts of the provinces of Tarragona and Lleida.[^cite_note-106]

In the Mediterranean area, summers are dry and hot with sea breezes, and the maximum temperature is around 26–31 °C (79–88 °F). Winter is cool or slightly cold depending on the location. It snows frequently in the Pyrenees, and it occasionally snows at lower altitudes, even by the coastline. Spring and autumn are typically the rainiest seasons, except for the Pyrenean valleys, where summer is typically stormy.

The inland part of Catalonia is hotter and drier in summer. Temperature may reach 35 °C (95 °F), some days even 40 °C (104 °F). Nights are cooler there than at the coast, with the temperature of around 14–17 °C (57–63 °F). Fog is not uncommon in valleys and plains; it can be especially persistent, with [freezing drizzle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freezing_drizzle) episodes and subzero temperatures during winter, mainly along the [Ebro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebro) and [Segre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Segre_River) valleys and in the [Plain of Vic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plain_of_Vic). On February 2, 1956, −32 °C (−26 °F) was recorded at Lake Gento in the province of Lleida, which is the lowest temperature ever recorded in Spain.[^cite_note-107]

### Topography

See also: [List of mountains in Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mountains_in_Catalonia)



[Geomorphologic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geomorphologic) map of Catalonia:&#x20;

- &#x20; [Pyrenees](24707.md)

- &#x20; [Pre-Pyrenees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Pyrenees)

- &#x20; [Catalan Central Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_Central_Depression)

- &#x20; Smaller mountain ranges of\
  &#x20;the Central Depression

- &#x20; [Catalan Transversal Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_Transversal_Range)

- &#x20; [Catalan Pre-Coastal Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_Pre-Coastal_Range)

- &#x20; [Catalan Coastal Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_Coastal_Range)

- &#x20; [Catalan Coastal Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_Coastal_Depression)\
  &#x20;and other coastal and pre-coastal plains



[Besiberri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Besiberri) in Catalan [Pyrenees](24707.md)&#x20;

Catalonia has a marked geographical diversity, considering the relatively small size of its territory. The [geography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography) is conditioned by the Mediterranean coast, with 580 kilometres (360 miles) of coastline, and the towering Pyrenees along the long northern border. Catalonia is divided into three main geomorphological units:[^cite_note-109]

- The Pyrenees: mountainous formation that connects the Iberian Peninsula with the [European continental territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Europe) (see passage above);

- The Catalan Coastal mountain ranges or the Catalan Mediterranean System: an alternating elevations and planes parallel to the Mediterranean coast;

- The [Catalan Central Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_Central_Depression): structural unit which forms the eastern sector of the Valley of the Ebro.



Mountain of [Montserrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montserrat_\(mountain\)) and the [monastery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Maria_de_Montserrat_Abbey)

The Catalan Pyrenees represent almost half the length of the Pyrenees, as they extend more than 200 kilometres (120 miles). They are traditionally differentiated into two parts, the Axial Pyrenees (the main part) and the [Pre-Pyrenees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Pyrenees) (southern from the Axial) which are mountainous formations parallel to the main mountain ranges but with lower altitudes, less steep and a different geological formation. The highest mountain of Catalonia, located north of the comarca of Pallars Sobirà, is the Pica d'Estats (3,143 m), followed by the Puigpedrós (2,914 m). The [Serra del Cadí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serra_del_Cad%C3%AD) comprises the highest peaks in the Pre-Pyrenees and forms the southern boundary of the [Cerdanya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cerdanya) valley.

The Central Catalan Depression is a plain located between the Pyrenees and Pre-Coastal Mountains. Elevation ranges from 200 to 600 metres (660 to 1,970 feet). The plains and the water that descend from the Pyrenees have made it fertile territory for [agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture) and numerous irrigation canals have been built. Another major plain is the [Empordà](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empord%C3%A0), located in the northeast.

The Catalan Mediterranean system is based on two ranges running roughly parallel to the coast (southwest–northeast), called the Coastal and the Pre-Coastal Ranges. The Coastal Range is both the shorter and the lower of the two, while the Pre-Coastal is greater in both length and elevation. Areas within the Pre-Coastal Range include [Montserrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montserrat_\(mountain\)), [Montseny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montseny_Massif) and the [Ports de Tortosa-Beseit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ports_de_Tortosa-Beseit). Lowlands alternate with the Coastal and Pre-Coastal Ranges. The Coastal Lowland is located to the East of the Coastal Range between it and the coast, while the Pre-Coastal Lowlands are located inland, between the Coastal and Pre-Coastal Ranges, and includes the [Vallès](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vall%C3%A8s) and [Penedès](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pened%C3%A8s) plains.

### Flora and fauna



Montseny brook newt (*[Calotriton arnoldi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calotriton_arnoldi)*), [endemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endemic) to the [Montseny Massif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montseny_Massif)

Catalonia is a showcase of European [landscapes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landscapes) on a small scale. Just over 30,000 square kilometres (12,000 square miles) hosting a variety of substrates, soils, climates, directions, altitudes and distances to the sea. The area is of great ecological diversity and a remarkable wealth of landscapes, habitats and species.

The fauna of Catalonia comprises a minority of animals endemic to the region and a majority of non-endemic animals. Much of Catalonia enjoys a Mediterranean climate (except mountain areas), which makes many of the animals that live there adapted to Mediterranean ecosystems. Of mammals, there are plentiful [wild boar](52255.md), red foxes, as well as [roe deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_deer) and in the Pyrenees, the [Pyrenean chamois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyrenean_chamois). Other large species such as the [bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bear) have been recently reintroduced.

The waters of the [Balearic Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balearic_Sea) are rich in biodiversity, and even the [megafaunas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megafauna) of the oceans; various types of whales (such as [fin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fin_whale), [sperm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sperm_whale), and [pilot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long-finned_pilot_whale)) and [dolphins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dolphin) can be found in the area.[^cite_note-110][^cite_note-111]

### Hydrography

See also: [List of rivers of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_rivers_of_Catalonia)



[Lake of Banyoles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_of_Banyoles)&#x20;



[Tossa de Mar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tossa_de_Mar), Costa Brava

Most of Catalonia belongs to the [Mediterranean Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_Basin). The Catalan hydrographic network consists of two important basins, that of the [Ebro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebro) and the one that comprises the internal basins of Catalonia (respectively covering 46.84% and 51.43% of the territory), all of them flow to the [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean). Furthermore, there is the [Garona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garona) river basin that flows to the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md), but it only covers 1.73% of the Catalan territory.

The hydrographic network can be divided in two sectors, an occidental slope or Ebro river slope and one oriental slope constituted by minor rivers that flow to the Mediterranean along the Catalan coast. The first slope provides an average of 18,700 [cubic hectometres](30125638.md) (4.5 [cubic miles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cubic_mile)) per year, while the second only provides an average of 2,020 hm<sup>3</sup> (0.48 cu mi)/year. The difference is due to the big contribution of the Ebro river, from which the [Segre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Segre_\(river\)) is an important tributary. Moreover, in Catalonia there is a relative wealth of groundwaters, although there is inequality between *[comarques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comarca)*, given the complex geological structure of the territory.[^cite_note-GEC_clima-112] In the Pyrenees there are many small lakes, remnants of the [ice age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quaternary_glaciation). The biggest are the [lake of Banyoles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_of_Banyoles) and the recently recovered [lake of Ivars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_of_Ivars?action=edit\&redlink=1).

The Catalan coast is almost rectilinear, with a length of 580 kilometres (360 mi) and few [landforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landform)—the most relevant are the [Cap de Creus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cap_de_Creus) and the [Gulf of Roses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Roses) to the north and the [Ebro Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebro_Delta) to the south. The [Catalan Coastal Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_Coastal_Range) hugs the coastline, and it is split into two segments, one between [L'Estartit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L'Estartit) and the town of [Blanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blanes) (the [Costa Brava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costa_Brava)), and the other at the south, at the Costes del [Garraf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garraf).

The principal [rivers in Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_rivers_of_Catalonia) are the [Ter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ter_\(river\)), [Llobregat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Llobregat), and the [Ebro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebro) (Catalan: *Ebre*), all of which run into the [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean).

### Anthropic pressure and protection of nature

The majority of Catalan population is concentrated in 30% of the territory, mainly in the coastal plains. Intensive agriculture, livestock farming and industrial activities have been accompanied by a massive [tourist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_tourism) influx (more than 20 million annual visitors), a rate of urbanisation and even of major metropolisation which has led to a strong urban sprawl: two thirds of Catalans live in the urban area of Barcelona, while the proportion of urban land increased from 4.2% in 1993 to 6.2% in 2009, a growth of 48.6% in sixteen years, complemented with a dense network of transport infrastructure. This is accompanied by a certain agricultural abandonment (decrease of 15% of all areas cultivated in Catalonia between 1993 and 2009) and a global threat to the [natural environment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_environment). Human activities have also put some animal species at risk, or even led to their disappearance from the territory, like the [gray wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gray_wolf) and probably the [brown bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_bear) of the Pyrenees. The pressure created by this model of life means that the country's [ecological footprint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_footprint) exceeds its administrative area.[^cite_note-113]

Faced with these problems, Catalan authorities initiated several measures whose purpose is to protect natural ecosystems. Thus, in 1990, the Catalan government created the Nature Conservation Council (Catalan: *Consell de Protecció de la Natura*), an advisory body with the aim to study, protect and manage the natural environments and landscapes of Catalonia. In addition, the Generalitat has carried out the Plan of Spaces of Natural Interest (*Pla d'Espais d'Interès Natural* or PEIN) in 1992 while eighteen Natural Spaces of Special Protection (*Espais Naturals de Protecció Especial* or ENPE) have been instituted.

There is a [National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Park), [Aigüestortes i Estany de Sant Maurici](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aig%C3%BCestortes_i_Estany_de_Sant_Maurici_National_Park); fourteen [Natural Parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_park_\(Spain\)), [Alt Pirineu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alt_Pirineu_Natural_Park), Aiguamolls de l'Empordà, [Cadí-Moixeró](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cad%C3%AD-Moixer%C3%B3_Natural_Park), [Cap de Creus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cap_de_Creus), Sources of Ter and Freser, [Collserola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collserola), [Ebro Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ebro_Delta), [Ports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ports_de_Tortosa-Beseit), [Montgrí, Medes Islands and Lower Ter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgr%C3%AD,_Medes_Islands_and_Lower_Ter_Natural_Park), [Montseny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montseny_Massif), [Montserrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montserrat_\(mountain\)), Sant Llorenç del Munt and l'Obac, [Serra de Montsant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serra_de_Montsant), and the [Garrotxa Volcanic Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zona_Volc%C3%A0nica_de_la_Garrotxa_Natural_Park); as well as three Natural Places of National Interest (*Paratge Natural d'Interes Nacional* or PNIN), the [Pedraforca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedraforca), the [Poblet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poblet_Monastery) Forest and the [Albères](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alb%C3%A8res).
