Main articles: [Politics of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Catalonia), [Politics of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Spain), and [Catalan independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_independence)



[Lluís Companys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Llu%C3%ADs_Companys), second president of the Generalitat of Catalonia between 1933 and 1940, executed by Franco's regime

After Franco's death in 1975 and the adoption of a democratic constitution in Spain in 1978, Catalonia recovered and extended the powers that it had gained in the [Statute of Autonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Autonomy) of 1932[^cite_note-114] but lost with the fall of the [Second Spanish Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Spanish_Republic)[^cite_note-115] at the end of the [Spanish Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Civil_War) in 1939.

This autonomous community has gradually achieved more [autonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-governance) since the approval of the [Spanish Constitution of 1978](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Constitution_of_1978). The Generalitat holds exclusive jurisdiction in education, health, culture, environment, communications, transportation, commerce, public safety and local government, and only shares jurisdiction with the Spanish government in justice.[^cite_note-116] In all, some analysts argue that formally the current system grants Catalonia with "more self-government than almost any other corner in Europe".[^cite_note-117]

The support for Catalan nationalism ranges from a demand for further autonomy and the [federalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalisation) of Spain to the desire for independence from the rest of Spain, expressed by Catalan independentists.[^cite_note-CEO_poll-118] The first survey following the Constitutional Court ruling that cut back elements of the 2006 Statute of Autonomy, published by *[La Vanguardia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Vanguardia)* on 18 July 2010, found that 46% of the voters would support independence in a referendum.[^cite_note-La_Vanguardia_poll-119] In February of the same year, a poll by the [Open University of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_University_of_Catalonia) gave more or less the same results.[^cite_note-120] Other polls have shown lower support for independence, ranging from 40 to 49%.[^cite_note-Cadenaser.com-121][^cite_note-auto-122][^cite_note-Lavanguardia.es-123] Although it is established in the whole of the territory, support for independence is significantly higher in the hinterland and the northeast, away from the more populous coastal areas such as Barcelona.[^cite_note-eldiario.es-124]

Since 2011 when the question started to be regularly surveyed by the governmental Center for Public Opinion Studies (CEO), support for Catalan independence has been on the rise.[^cite_note-125] According to the CEO opinion poll from July 2016, 47.7% of Catalans would vote for independence and 42.4% against it while, about the question of preferences, according to the CEO opinion poll from March 2016, a 57.2 claim to be "absolutely" or "fairly" in favour of independence.[^cite_note-126][^cite_note-127] Other polls have shown lower support for independence, ranging from 40 to 49%.[^cite_note-Cadenaser.com-121][^cite_note-auto-122][^cite_note-Lavanguardia.es-123] Other polls show more variable results, according with the Spanish CIS, as of December 2016, 47% of Catalans rejected independence and 45% supported it.[^cite_note-antena3.com-128]

In hundreds of non-binding local referendums on independence, organised across Catalonia from 13 September 2009, a large majority voted for independence, although critics argued that the polls were mostly held in pro-independence areas. In December 2009, 94% of those voting backed independence from Spain, on a turn-out of 25%.[^cite_note-129] The final local referendum was held in Barcelona, in April 2011. On 11 September 2012, a pro-independence march pulled in a crowd of between 600,000 (according to the [Spanish Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Government)), 1.5 million (according to the [Guàrdia Urbana de Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gu%C3%A0rdia_Urbana_de_Barcelona)), and 2 million (according to its promoters);[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-131] whereas poll results revealed that half the population of Catalonia supported secession from Spain.

Prominent Catalan politicians in [Spain](26667.md).



[Juan Prim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Prim,_1st_Marquis_of_los_Castillejos) ([Spanish prime minister](203556.md) under regent [don Francisco Serrano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Serrano,_1st_Duke_of_la_Torre))



[Estanislao Figueras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estanislao_Figueras) (president of the [First Spanish Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Spanish_Republic))



[Francesc Pi i Margall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francesc_Pi_i_Margall)\
(president of the [First Spanish Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Spanish_Republic))

Two major factors were [Spain's Constitutional Court's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Spain) 2010 decision to declare part of the 2006 Statute of Autonomy of Catalonia unconstitutional, as well as the fact that Catalonia contributes 19.49% of the central government's tax revenue, but only receives 14.03% of central government's spending.[^cite_note-132]

Parties that consider themselves either Catalan nationalist or [independentist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_independence) have been present in all Catalan governments since 1980. The largest Catalan nationalist party, [Convergence and Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convergence_and_Union), ruled Catalonia from 1980 to 2003,[^cite_note-Encyclopedia_Britannica-133] and returned to power in the [2010 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Catalan_regional_election).[^cite_note-Encyclopedia_Britannica-133] Between 2003 and 2010, a leftist coalition, composed by the [Catalan Socialists' Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partit_dels_Socialistes_de_Catalunya), the pro-independence[^cite_note-134] [Republican Left of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Left_of_Catalonia) and the leftist-environmentalist [Initiative for Catalonia-Greens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iniciativa_per_Catalunya_Verds),[^cite_note-135] implemented policies that widened Catalan autonomy.[^cite_note-136]

In the [25 November 2012 Catalan parliamentary election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Catalonian_parliamentary_election), sovereigntist parties supporting a secession referendum gathered 59.01% of the votes and held 87 of the 135 seats in the Catalan Parliament. Parties supporting independence from the rest of Spain obtained 49.12% of the votes and a majority of 74 seats.

[Artur Mas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artur_Mas), then the president of Catalonia, organised early elections that took place on 27 September 2015. In these elections, Convergència and Esquerra Republicana decided to join, and they presented themselves under the coalition named [Junts pel Sí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junts_pel_S%C3%AD) (in [Catalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_language), Together for Yes). Junts pel Sí won 62 seats and was the most voted party, and CUP ([Candidatura d'Unitat Popular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candidatura_d'Unitat_Popular), a far-left and independentist party) won another 10, so the sum of all the independentist forces/parties was 72 seats, reaching an absolute majority, but not in number of individual votes, comprising 47,74% of the total.[^cite_note-137]

### Statute of Autonomy

Main article: [Statute of Autonomy of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Autonomy_of_Catalonia)

See also: [Autonomous communities of Spain](55002.md) and [Nationalities and regions of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalities_and_regions_of_Spain)



The first [Statute of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Catalonia), 1932

The [Statute of Autonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Autonomy) of Catalonia is the fundamental organic law, second only to the Spanish Constitution from which the Statute originates.

In the [Spanish Constitution of 1978](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Constitution_of_1978) Catalonia, along with the [Basque Country](20514897.md) and [Galicia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galicia_\(Spain\)), was defined as a "[nationality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalities_and_regions_of_Spain)". The same constitution gave Catalonia the automatic right to autonomy, which resulted in the [Statute of Autonomy of Catalonia of 1979](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Autonomy_of_Catalonia_of_1979).

Both the 1979 Statute of Autonomy and the current one, approved in 2006, state that "Catalonia, as a nationality, exercises its self-government constituted as an Autonomous Community in accordance with the Constitution and with the Statute of Autonomy of Catalonia, which is its basic institutional law, always under the law in Spain".[^cite_note-138]

The Preamble of the 2006 Statute of Autonomy of Catalonia states that the [Parliament of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Catalonia) has defined Catalonia as a [nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation), but that "the Spanish Constitution recognises Catalonia's national reality as a nationality".[^cite_note-139] While the Statute was approved by and sanctioned by both the Catalan and Spanish parliaments, and later by referendum in Catalonia, it has been subject to a legal challenge by the surrounding autonomous communities of [Aragon](39443.md), [Balearic Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balearic_Islands) and [Valencia](20514168.md),[^cite_note-140] as well as by the conservative [People's Party](404727.md). The objections are based on various issues such as disputed [cultural heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_heritage) but, especially, on the Statute's alleged breaches of the principle of "solidarity between regions" in fiscal and educational matters enshrined by the [Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Constitution_of_1978).[^cite_note-141]

Spain's [Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Spain) assessed the disputed articles and on 28 June 2010, issued its judgment on the principal allegation of unconstitutionality presented by the People's Party in 2006. The judgment granted clear passage to 182 articles of the 223 that make up the fundamental text. The court approved 73 of the 114 articles that the People's Party had contested, while declaring 14 articles unconstitutional in whole or in part and imposing a restrictive interpretation on 27 others.[^cite_note-142] The court accepted the specific provision that described Catalonia as a "nation", however ruled that it was a historical and cultural term with no legal weight, and that Spain remained the only nation recognised by the constitution.[^cite_note-143][^cite_note-144][^cite_note-145][^cite_note-146]

### Government and law

Main article: [Generalitat of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generalitat_of_Catalonia)



[Palace of the Parliament of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_the_Parliament_of_Catalonia), located in [Ciutadella park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ciutadella_park), Barcelona



[Palau de la Generalitat de Catalunya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palau_de_la_Generalitat_de_Catalunya), Barcelona, headquarters of the President and the Government of Catalonia

The Catalan Statute of Autonomy establishes that Catalonia, as an autonomous community, is organised politically through the [Generalitat of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generalitat_of_Catalonia) (Catalan: *Generalitat de Catalunya*), confirmed by the [Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Catalonia), the Presidency of the Generalitat, the [Government](294461.md) or Executive Council and the other institutions established by the Parliament, among them the Ombudsman (*[Síndic de Greuges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%ADndic_de_Greuges_\(Catalonia\)?action=edit\&redlink=1)*), the Office of Auditors (*Sindicatura de Comptes*) the Council for Statutory Guarantees (*Consell de Garanties Estatutàries*) or the Audiovisual Council of Catalonia (*Consell de l'Audiovisual de Catalunya*).



[Salvador Illa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador_Illa), [President of the Generalitat of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_Government_of_Catalonia) (2024–)

The [Parliament of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Catalonia) (Catalan: *Parlament de Catalunya*) is the unicameral legislative body of the Generalitat and represents the people of Catalonia. Its 135 members (*diputats*) are elected by [universal suffrage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_suffrage) to serve for a four-year period. According to the Statute of Autonomy, it has powers to legislate over devolved matters such as education, health, culture, internal institutional and territorial organisation, nominate the President of the Generalitat, and control the Government, budget and other affairs. The last Catalan election [was held on 12 May 2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Catalan_regional_election), and the current speaker (president) of the parliament is [Josep Rull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josep_Rull), incumbent since 10 June 2024.

The [President of the Generalitat of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_Generalitat_of_Catalonia) (Catalan: *president de la Generalitat de Catalunya*) is the highest representative of Catalonia, and is also responsible for leading the government's action, presiding over the Executive Council. Since the restoration of the Generalitat on the return of democracy in Spain, the [Presidents of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Presidents_of_the_Generalitat_de_Catalunya) have been [Josep Tarradellas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josep_Tarradellas) (1977–1980, president in exile since 1954), [Jordi Pujol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordi_Pujol) (1980–2003), [Pasqual Maragall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasqual_Maragall) (2003–2006), [José Montilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Montilla) (2006–2010), [Artur Mas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artur_Mas) (2010–2016), [Carles Puigdemont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carles_Puigdemont) (2016–2017) and, after the imposition of direct rule from Madrid, [Quim Torra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quim_Torra) (2018–2020), [Pere Aragonès](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pere_Aragon%C3%A8s) (2021–2024) and Salvador Illa (2024–).

The [Executive Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Council_of_Catalonia) (Catalan: *Consell Executiu*) or Government (*Govern*), is the body responsible of the government of the Generalitat. It holds executive and regulatory power, being accountable to the Catalan Parliament. The Executive Council comprises the President of the Generalitat, the [First Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_First_Ministers_of_Catalonia) (*conseller primer*) or the [Vice President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Vice_Presidents_of_Catalonia), and the ministers (*consellers*) appointed by the president. Its seat is the [Palau de la Generalitat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palau_de_la_Generalitat_de_Catalunya), Barcelona. In 2025 the government composed of solely one party, the [Socialists' Party of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialists'_Party_of_Catalonia) (PSC-PSOE) and is made up of 16 ministers, including the vice President, alongside the president and a secretary of government.

### Security forces and Justice

Main article: [Mossos d'Esquadra](294120.md)

Catalonia has its own police force, the *[Mossos d'Esquadra](294120.md)* (officially called *Mossos d'Esquadra-Policia de la Generalitat de Catalunya*), whose origins date back to the 18th century. Since 1980 they have been under the command of the Generalitat, and since 1994 they have expanded in number in order to replace the national [Civil Guard](325008.md) and [National Police Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Police_Corps), which report directly to the Homeland Department of Spain. The national bodies retain personnel within Catalonia to exercise functions of national scope such as overseeing ports, airports, coasts, international borders, custom offices, the identification of documents and arms control, immigration control, terrorism prevention, arms trafficking prevention, amongst others.

Most of the justice system is administered by national judicial institutions, the highest body and last judicial instance in the Catalan jurisdiction, integrating the Spanish judiciary, is the [High Court of Justice of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Court_of_Justice_of_Catalonia). The [criminal justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criminal_justice) system is uniform throughout Spain, while [civil law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_law_\(legal_system\)) is administered separately within Catalonia. The civil laws that are subject to autonomous legislation have been codified in the [Civil Code of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Code_of_Catalonia) (*Codi civil de Catalunya*) since 2002.[^cite_note-147]

Catalonia, together with [Navarre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navarre) and the [Basque Country](20514897.md), are the Spanish communities with the highest degree of autonomy in terms of [law enforcement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_enforcement_agency).

### Administrative divisions



Provinces, regions and counties of Catalonia (until 2015)

Main article: [Subdivisions of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subdivisions_of_Catalonia)

See also: [Political divisions of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_divisions_of_Spain) and [Local government in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_Spain)

Catalonia is organised territorially into [provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provinces_of_Spain) or [regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vegueries_of_Catalonia), further subdivided into [comarques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comarca) and [municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipality). The [2006 Statute of Autonomy of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Autonomy_of_Catalonia_of_2006) establishes the administrative organisation of the later three.

#### Provinces

Further information: [Provinces of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provinces_of_Spain)

Much like the rest of Spain, Catalonia is divided administratively into four provinces, the governing body of which is the [Provincial Deputation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provincial_deputation_\(Spain\)) (Catalan: *Diputació Provincial*, Occitan: *Deputacion Provinciau*, Spanish: *Diputación Provincial*). As of 2010, the four provinces and their populations were:[^cite_note-148]

- [Province of Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Barcelona): 5,701,708 population

- [Province of Girona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Girona): 777,258 population

- [Province of Lleida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Lleida): 437,939 population

- [Province of Tarragona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Tarragona): 830,804 population

Unlike vegueries, provinces do not follow the limitations of the subdivisional counties, notably [Baixa Cerdanya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cerdanya_\(comarca\)), which is split in half between the demarcations of Lleida and Girona. This situation has led some isolated municipalities to request province changes from the Spanish government.[^cite_note-149]

#### Vegueries

Main article: [Vegueries of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vegueries_of_Catalonia)

Besides provinces, Catalonia is internally divided into eight regions or vegueries, based on the feudal administrative territorial jurisdiction of the [Principality of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Catalonia).[^cite_note-\:1-150] Established in 2006, vegueries are used by the [Generalitat de Catalunya](294461.md) with the aim to more effectively divide Catalonia administratively. In addition, vegueries are intended to become Catalonia's first-level administrative division and a full replacement for the four deputations of the Catalan provinces, creating a council for each vegueria,[^cite_note-\:2-151][^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153] but this has not been realised as changes to the statewide provinces system are unconstitutional without a constitutional amendment.[^cite_note-\:3-154]

The territorial plan of Catalonia (*Pla territorial general de Catalunya*) provided six general functional areas,[^cite_note-155] but was amended by Law 24/2001, of 31 December, recognizing *Alt Pirineu and Aran* as a new functional area differentiated of Ponent.[^cite_note-156] After some opposition from some territories, it was made possible for the Aran Valley to retain its government (the vegueria is renamed to *Alt Pirineu*, although the name *Alt Pirineu and Aran* is still used by the regional plan)[^cite_note-157] and in 2016, the Catalan Parliament approved the eighth vegueria, Penedès, split from the Barcelona region.[^cite_note-\:4-158][^cite_note-\:1-150]

As of 2022, the eight regions and their populations were:

- [Alt Pirineu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alt_Pirineu_i_Aran) (capital [La Seu d'Urgell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Seu_d'Urgell)): 63,892 population

- [Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%80mbit_metropolit%C3%A0_de_Barcelona) (capital [Barcelona](4443.md)): 4,916,847 population

- [Camp de Tarragona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_de_Tarragona) (capital [Tarragona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarragona)): 536,453 population

- [Central Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalunya_Central) (capital [Manresa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manresa)): 413,349 population

- [Girona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comarques_Gironines) (capital [Girona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Girona)): 761,690 population

- [Ponent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ponent) (capital [Lleida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lleida)): 365,289 population

- [Penedès](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pened%C3%A8s_\(division\)) (capital [Vilanova i la Geltrú](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilanova_i_la_Geltr%C3%BA)): 497,764 population

- [Terres de l'Ebre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terres_de_l'Ebre) (capital [Tortosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tortosa)): 182,231 population

- [Aran Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Val_d'Aran) (capital [Vielha e Mijaran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vielha_e_Mijaran)): 10,194 population (The autonomous Aran Valley, considered a "unique territorial entity", is not part of any vegueria.)

#### Comarques

Main article: [Comarques of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comarques_of_Catalonia)

Comarques (often known as *counties* in English, but different from the historical [Catalan counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_counties)[^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161]) are entities composed of municipalities to internally manage their responsibilities and services. The current regional division has its roots in a decree of the Generalitat de Catalunya of 1936, in effect until 1939, when it was suppressed by Franco. In 1987 the Catalan Government reestablished the comarcal division and in 1988 three new comarques were added ([Alta Ribagorça](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alta_Ribagor%C3%A7a), [Pla d'Urgell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pla_d'Urgell) and [Pla de l'Estany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pla_de_l'Estany)). Some further revisions have been realised since then, such as the additions of [Moianès](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moian%C3%A8s) and [Lluçanès](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Llu%C3%A7an%C3%A8s) counties, in 2015 and 2023 respectively. Except for [Barcelonès](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barcelon%C3%A8s), every comarca is administered by a [comarcal council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comarcal_council) (*consell comarcal*).

As of 2024, Catalonia is divided into 42 counties plus the [Aran Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Val_d'Aran). The latter, although previously (and still informally) considered a comarca, obtained in 1990 a particular status within Catalonia due to its differences in culture and language, being administered by a body known as the *[Conselh Generau d'Aran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conselh_Generau_d'Aran)* (General Council of Aran), and in 2015 it was defined as a "unique territorial entity" instead of a county.[^cite_note-162]

#### Municipalities

Further information: [Municipalities of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipalities_of_Catalonia)

There are at present 947 municipalities (*municipis*) in Catalonia. Each municipality is run by a council (*ajuntament*) elected every four years by the residents in local elections. The council consists of a number of members (*regidors*) depending on population, who elect the [mayor](101146.md) (*alcalde* or *batlle*). Its seat is the [town hall](383746.md) (*ajuntament*, *casa de la ciutat* or *casa de la vila*).

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Spain](27393.md)



Peach fields in [Aitona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aitona)





Wines and *_[caves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cava_\(Spanish_wine\))_* with [denomination of origin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denominaci%C3%B3n_de_origen)



[Protected denominations of origin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denominaci%C3%B3n_de_origen) of [olive oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olive_oil)



[Costa Brava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costa_Brava) beach. [Tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Spain) plays an important role in the Catalan economy.

With a highly industrialised and populated region, the nominal [GDP](12594.md) of Catalonia in 2024 was €302 billion, ranking second after [Madrid](69802.md) (€316 billion) and with a per capita GDP of €37,477.[^cite_note-163] The [median income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_income) was €27,132 in 2024, ranking 4th [among the rest of autonomous communities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Spanish_autonomous_communities_by_median_income).[^cite_note-164]

Catalonia's long-term credit rating is BB (Non-Investment Grade) according to [Standard & Poor's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_&_Poor's), Ba2 (Non-Investment Grade) according to [Moody's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moody's), and BBB- (Low Investment Grade) according to [Fitch Ratings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fitch_Ratings).[^cite_note-165][^cite_note-166][^cite_note-agency-ratings-167] Catalonia's rating is tied for worst with between 1 and 5 other autonomous communities of Spain, depending on the rating agency.[^cite_note-agency-ratings-167]

According to a 2020 study by Eu-Starts-Up, the Catalan capital is one of the European bases of "reference for start-ups" and the fifth city in the world to establish one of these companies, behind London, Berlin, Paris and Amsterdam. Barcelona is behind London, New York, Paris, Moscow, Tokyo, Dubai and Singapore and ahead of Los Angeles and Madrid.[^cite_note-168]

In the context of the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis), Catalonia was expected to suffer a [recession](25382.md) amounting to almost a 2% contraction of its regional GDP in 2009.[^cite_note-169] Catalonia's debt in 2012 was the highest of all Spain's autonomous communities,[^cite_note-170] reaching €13,476 million, i.e. 38% of the total debt of the 17 autonomous communities,[^cite_note-171] but in recent years its economy recovered a positive evolution and the GDP grew a 3.3% in 2015.[^cite_note-172]



Industrial park in [Castellbisbal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castellbisbal)



Factories, [La Pobla de Mafumet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Pobla_de_Mafumet), Tarragona

Catalonia is amongst the [List of country subdivisions by GDP over 100 billion US dollars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_country_subdivisions_by_GDP_over_100_billion_US_dollars) and is a member of the [Four Motors for Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Motors_for_Europe) organisation.

The distribution of sectors is as follows:[^cite_note-173]

- [Primary sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_sector_of_industry): 3%. The amount of land devoted to agricultural use is 33%.

- [Secondary sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_sector_of_industry): 37% (compared to Spain's 29%)

- [Tertiary sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_sector_of_industry): 60% (compared to Spain's 67%)

The main tourist destinations in Catalonia are the city of Barcelona, the beaches of the Costa Brava in [Girona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Girona), the beaches of the [Costa del Maresme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costa_del_Maresme) and [Costa del Garraf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costa_del_Garraf?action=edit\&redlink=1) from [Malgrat de Mar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malgrat_de_Mar) to [Vilanova i la Geltrú](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vilanova_i_la_Geltr%C3%BA) and the [Costa Daurada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costa_Daurada) in Tarragona. In the High Pyrenees there are several ski resorts, near [Lleida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lleida). On 1 November 2012, Catalonia started charging a tourist tax.[^cite_note-174] The revenue is used to promote tourism, and to maintain and upgrade tourism-related infrastructure.



Eix Macià, [Sabadell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabadell)&#x20;

Many of Spain's leading [savings banks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savings_bank) were based in Catalonia before the [independence referendum of 2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Catalan_independence_referendum). However, in the aftermath of the referendum, many of them moved their registered office to other parts of Spain. That includes the two biggest Catalan banks at that moment, [La Caixa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Caixa), which moved its office to [Palma de Mallorca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palma_de_Mallorca), and [Banc Sabadell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banc_Sabadell), ranked fourth among all Spanish private banks and which moved its office to [Alicante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alicante).[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176] That happened after the Spanish government passed a law allowing companies to move their registered office without requiring the approval of the company's general meeting of shareholders.[^cite_note-177] Overall, there was a negative net relocation rate of companies based in Catalonia moving to other autonomous communities of Spain. From the 2017 independence referendum until the end of 2018, for example, Catalonia lost 5454 companies to other parts of Spain (mainly Madrid), 2359 only in 2018, gaining 467 new ones from the rest of the country during 2018.[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179] It has been reported that the Spanish government and the Spanish King [Felipe VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felipe_VI) pressured some of the big Catalan companies to move their headquarters outside of the region.[^cite_note-180][^cite_note-181]

The stock market of Barcelona, which in 2016 had a volume of around €152 billion, is the second largest of Spain after Madrid, and [Fira de Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fira_de_Barcelona) organises international exhibitions and congresses to do with different sectors of the economy.[^cite_note-182]

The main economic cost for Catalan families is the purchase of a home. According to data from the Society of Appraisal on 31 December 2005 Catalonia is, after Madrid, the second most expensive region in Spain for housing: 3,397 €/m<sup>2</sup> on average (see [Spanish property bubble](5259909.md)).

### Unemployment

The unemployment rate stood at 8.4% in 2025 and was lower than the national average.[^cite_note-183]

### Transport

#### Airports



[Barcelona Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barcelona_El_Prat_Airport) tower

See also: [List of airports in Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Catalonia)

Airports in Catalonia are owned and operated by [Aena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aena) (a [Spanish Government](1306113.md) entity) except two airports in Lleida which are operated by [Aeroports de Catalunya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aeroports_de_Catalunya) (an entity belonging to the [Government of Catalonia](294461.md)).

- [Barcelona El Prat Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barcelona_El_Prat_Airport) (Aena)

- [Girona-Costa Brava Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Girona-Costa_Brava_Airport) (Aena)

- [Reus Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reus_Airport) (Aena)

- [Lleida-Alguaire Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lleida-Alguaire_Airport) (Aeroports de Catalunya)

- [Sabadell Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabadell_Airport) (Aena)

- [La Seu d'Urgell Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Seu_d'Urgell_Airport) (Aeroports de Catalunya)

#### Ports

See also: [List of ports in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ports_in_Spain)



Aerial view of Zona Franca and the Port of Barcelona

Since the Middle Ages, Catalonia has been well integrated into international maritime networks. The [port of Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Barcelona) (owned and operated by *[Puertos del Estado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puertos_del_Estado)*, a Spanish Government entity) is an industrial, commercial and tourist port of worldwide importance. With 1,950,000 [TEUs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-foot_equivalent_unit) in 2015, it is the first container port in Catalonia, the third in Spain after Valencia and [Algeciras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algeciras) in Andalusia, the 9th in the Mediterranean Sea, the 14th in Europe and the 68th in the world. It is the sixth largest cruise port in the world, and the largest in Europe and the Mediterranean with 2,364,292 passengers in 2014. The ports of [Tarragona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarragona) (owned and operated by Puertos del Estado) in the southwest and [Palamós](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palam%C3%B3s) near Girona to the northeast are smaller. The port of Palamós and the other ports in Catalonia (26) are operated and administered by *[Ports de la Generalitat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ports_de_la_Generalitat?action=edit\&redlink=1)*, a Catalan Government entity.

The development of these infrastructures, resulting from the topography and history of the Catalan territory, responds strongly to the administrative and political organisation of this autonomous community.

#### Roads

See also: [List of primary highways in Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_primary_highways_in_Catalonia)



[Autovia C-16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autov%C3%ADa_C-16) (*Eix del Llobregat*)

There are 12,000 kilometres (7,500 mi) of roads throughout Catalonia.

The principal highways are&#x20;**&#x20;AP-7&#x20;**&#x20;(*Autopista de la Mediterrània*) and&#x20;**&#x20;A-7&#x20;**&#x20;(*Autovia de la Mediterrània*). They follow the coast from the [French border](5843419.md) to [Valencia](20514168.md), [Murcia](20515376.md) and [Andalusia](2736.md). The main roads generally radiate from Barcelona. The&#x20;**&#x20;AP-2&#x20;**&#x20; (*Autopista del Nord-est*) and&#x20;**&#x20;A-2&#x20;**&#x20;(*Autovia del Nord-est*) connect inland and onward to Madrid.

Other major roads are:

Public-own roads in Catalonia are either managed by the autonomous government of Catalonia (e.g.,&#x20;**&#x20;C-&#x20;**&#x20;roads) or the [Spanish government](1306113.md) (e.g.,&#x20;**&#x20;AP-&#x20;**,&#x20;**&#x20;A-&#x20;**,&#x20;**&#x20;N-&#x20;**&#x20;roads).

#### Railways

See also: [Rail transport in Catalonia](22054993.md)



[High-speed train (AVE)](523382.md) at [Camp de Tarragona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_de_Tarragona)&#x20;

Catalonia saw the first railway construction in the [Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula) in 1848, linking Barcelona with [Mataró](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matar%C3%B3). Given the topography, most lines radiate from Barcelona. The city has both suburban and inter-city services. The main east coast line runs through the province connecting with the [SNCF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SNCF) (French Railways) at [Portbou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portbou) on the coast.

There are two publicly owned railway companies operating in Catalonia: the Catalan [FGC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferrocarrils_de_la_Generalitat_de_Catalunya) that operates commuter and regional services, and the Spanish national [Renfe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renfe) that operates long-distance and high-speed rail services (AVE and Avant) and the main commuter and regional service *[Rodalies de Catalunya](27342438.md)*, administered by the Catalan government since 2010.

[High-speed rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-speed_rail) ([AVE](523382.md)) services from [Madrid](41188263.md) currently reach Barcelona, via Lleida and Tarragona. The official opening between Barcelona and Madrid took place 20 February 2008. The journey between Barcelona and Madrid now takes about two-and-a-half hours. A connection to the [French high-speed TGV network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-speed_rail_in_France#Network) has been completed (called the [Perpignan–Barcelona high-speed rail line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perpignan%E2%80%93Barcelona_high-speed_rail_line)) and the Spanish AVE service began commercial services on the line 9 January 2013, later offering services to [Marseille](40888948.md) on their high speed network.[^cite_note-184][^cite_note-\:0-185] This was shortly followed by the commencement of commercial service by the French TGV on 17 January 2013, leading to an average travel time on the [Paris-Barcelona TGV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_TGV_services#International) route of 7h 42m.[^cite_note-\:0-185][^cite_note-186] This new line passes through [Girona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Girona) and [Figueres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Figueres) with a [tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perthus_Tunnel) through the Pyrenees.

As of 2024, the official population of Catalonia was 8,067,454.[^cite_note-popcat-188] 1,194,947 residents did not have Spanish citizenship, accounting for about 16% of the population.[^cite_note-189]

The [Urban Region of Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_Region_of_Barcelona) includes 5,217,864 people and covers an area of 2,268 km<sup>2</sup> (876 sq mi). The metropolitan area of the Urban Region includes cities such as [L'Hospitalet de Llobregat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L'Hospitalet_de_Llobregat), [Sabadell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabadell), [Terrassa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrassa), [Badalona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badalona), [Santa Coloma de Gramenet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Coloma_de_Gramenet) and [Cornellà de Llobregat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornell%C3%A0_de_Llobregat).

In 1900, the population of Catalonia was 1,966,382 people and in 1970 it was 5,122,567.[^cite_note-popcat-188] The sizeable increase of the population was due to the demographic boom in Spain during the 1960s and early 1970s[^cite_note-190] as well as in consequence of large-scale internal migration from the rural economically weak regions to its more prospering industrial cities. In Catalonia, that wave of internal migration arrived from several regions of Spain, especially from [Andalusia](2736.md), [Murcia](20515376.md)[^cite_note-REUTCATPOP-191] and [Extremadura](68204.md).[^cite_note-192] As of 1999, it was estimated that over 60% of Catalans descended from 20th century migrations from other parts of Spain.[^cite_note-193]

Immigrants from other countries settled in Catalonia since the 1990s;[^cite_note-194] a large percentage comes from [Africa](5334607.md), [Latin America](18524.md) and [Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe), and smaller numbers from [Asia](689.md) and [Southern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Europe), often settling in urban centers such as Barcelona and industrial areas.[^cite_note-195] In 2017, Catalonia had 940,497 foreign residents (11.9% of the total population) with non-Spanish ID cards, without including those who acquired Spanish citizenship.[^cite_note-196]

### Religion

Religion in Catalonia (2020):

[^cite_note-Barometer2020-199]

1. [Roman Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholicism) (53.0%)

2. [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) (7.00%)

3. [Eastern Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy) (1.30%)

4. [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah's_Witnesses) (1.00%)

5. [Atheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism) (18.6%)

6. [Agnosticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agnosticism) (8.80%)

7. [Islam](6037917.md) (4.30%)

8. Other Religions (3.40%)

9. No answer/do not know (2.60%)

Main article: [Religion in Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Catalonia)

Historically, all the Catalan population was [Christian](5211.md), specifically [Catholic](4513503.md), but since the 1980s there has been a trend of decline of Christianity. Nevertheless, according to the most recent study sponsored by the [Government of Catalonia](294461.md), as of 2020, 62.3% of the Catalans identify as Christians (up from 61.9% in 2016[^cite_note-Barometer2016-200] and 56.5% in 2014[^cite_note-Barometer20142-201]) of whom 53.0% Catholics, 7.0% [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) and [Evangelicals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelicalism), 1.3% [Orthodox Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodoxy#Christianity) and 1.0% [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah's_Witnesses). At the same time, 18.6% of the population identify as [atheists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism), 8.8% as [agnostics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agnosticism), 4.3% as [Muslims](19541.md), and a further 3.4% as being of other religions.[^cite_note-Barometer2020-199]

See also: [Catalan language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_language), [Spanish language](26825.md), [Aranese dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aranese_dialect), and [Catalan Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_Sign_Language)

Main article: [Languages of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Catalonia)



[Catalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_language)-speaking lands of Europe (grey), often known as the [Catalan Countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_Countries)

Originating in the historic territory of Catalonia, [Catalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_language) is the official language of the Autonomous Community and has enjoyed special status since the approval of the [Statute of Autonomy of 1979](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Autonomy_of_1979) which declares it to be "Catalonia's own language",[^cite_note-article_6-203] a term which signifies a language given special legal status within a Spanish territory, or which is historically spoken within a given region. The other languages considered official in Catalonia are Spanish, which has official status throughout Spain, and Aranese Occitan, considered the "own language" of the [Val d'Aran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Val_d'Aran) territory. Given this, the sole official language for [toponymy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toponymy) throughout Catalonia is Catalan, except in the Val d'Aran where Occitan fulfils this role.[^cite_note-204]

According to the linguistic census held by the Government of Catalonia in 2013, Spanish is the most spoken language in Catalonia (46.53% claim Spanish as "their own language"), followed by [Catalan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_language) (37.26% claim Catalan as "their own language"). In everyday use, 11.95% of the population claim to use both languages equally, whereas 45.92% mainly use Spanish and 35.54% mainly use Catalan. There is a significant difference between the [Barcelona metropolitan area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barcelona_metropolitan_area) (and, to a lesser extent, the [Tarragona area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_de_Tarragona)), where Spanish is more spoken than Catalan, and the more rural and small town areas, where Catalan clearly prevails over Spanish.[^cite_note-205]

Since the [Statute of Autonomy of 1979](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Autonomy_of_1979), [Aranese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aranese) (a [Gascon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gascon_dialect) dialect of [Occitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occitan_language)) has also been official and subject to special protection in [Val d'Aran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Val_d'Aran). This small area of 7,000 inhabitants was the only place where a dialect of Occitan had received full official status. Then, on 9 August 2006, when the new Statute came into force, Occitan became official throughout Catalonia. Occitan is the mother tongue of 22.4% of the population of Val d'Aran, which has attracted heavy immigration from other Spanish regions to work in the service industry.[^cite_note-206] [Catalan Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_Sign_Language) is also officially recognised.[^cite_note-estatut_langs-18]

Although not considered an "official language" in the same way as Catalan, Spanish, and Occitan, the [Catalan Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_Sign_Language), with about 18,000 users in Catalonia,[^cite_note-207] is granted official recognition and support: "The public authorities shall guarantee the use of Catalan sign language and conditions of equality for deaf people who choose to use this language, which shall be the subject of education, protection and respect."[^cite_note-estatut_langs-18]

As was the case since the ascent of the Bourbon dynasty to the throne of Spain after the [War of the Spanish Succession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Spanish_Succession), and with the exception of the short period of the [Second Spanish Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Spanish_Republic), under [Francoist Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francoist_Spain) Catalan was banned from schools and all other official use, so that for example families were not allowed to officially register children with Catalan names.[^cite_note-208] During the Francoist period especially, the Spanish Government actively suppressed the Catalan language, criminalizing its public usage,[^cite_note-209] promoting both [diglossia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diglossia) and [linguistic substitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_shift).[^cite_note-210] Although never completely banned, Catalan language publishing was severely restricted during the early 1940s, with only religious texts and small-run self-published texts being released. Some books were published clandestinely or circumvented the restrictions by showing publishing dates prior to 1936.[^cite_note-211] This policy was changed in 1946, when restricted publishing in Catalan resumed.[^cite_note-212]

Rural–urban migration originating in other parts of Spain also reduced the social use of Catalan in urban areas and increased the use of Spanish. Lately, a similar [sociolinguistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociolinguistic) phenomenon has occurred with foreign immigration. Catalan cultural activity increased in the 1960s and the teaching of Catalan began thanks to the initiative of associations such as [Òmnium Cultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%92mnium_Cultural).

After the end of Francoist Spain, the newly established self-governing democratic institutions in Catalonia embarked on a long-term [language policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_policy) to recover the use of Catalan[^cite_note-213] and has, since 1983, enforced laws which attempt to protect and extend the use of Catalan. This policy, known as the "linguistic normalisation" (*normalització lingüística* in Catalan, *normalización lingüística* in Spanish) has been supported by the vast majority of Catalan political parties through the last thirty years. Some groups consider these efforts a way to discourage the use of Spanish,[^cite_note-214][^cite_note-215][^cite_note-216][^cite_note-217] whereas some others, including the Catalan government[^cite_note-218] and the European Union[^cite_note-219] consider the policies respectful,[^cite_note-220] or even as an example which "should be disseminated throughout the Union".[^cite_note-221]



Fragment of the *Greuges de Guirard Isarn* (c. 1080–1095), one of the earliest texts written almost completely in Catalan,

[^cite_note-222]

[^cite_note-223]

&#x20;predating the famous *[Homilies d'Organyà](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homilies_d'Organy%C3%A0)* by a century

Today, Catalan is the main language of the Catalan autonomous government and the other public institutions that fall under its jurisdiction. Basic public education is given mainly in Catalan, but also there are some hours per week of Spanish language instruction. Although the law requiring businesses to display all information (e.g. menus, posters) at least in Catalan is not systematically enforced, the majority of the [linguistic landscape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linguistic_landscape) in Catalonia is monolingually in Catalan. There is no obligation to display this information in either Occitan or Spanish, although there is no restriction on doing so in these or other languages and sometimes private businesses will display their information in Spanish or English as well. The use of fines was introduced in a 1997 linguistic law[^cite_note-224] that aims to increase the public use of Catalan and defend the rights of Catalan speakers. On the other hand, the Spanish Constitution does not recognise equal language rights for linguistic minorities since it enshrined Spanish as the only official language of the state, the knowledge of which being compulsory. Numerous laws regarding for instance the labelling of pharmaceutical products, make in effect Spanish the only language of compulsory use.

The law ensures that both Catalan and Spanish – being official languages – can be used by the citizens without prejudice in all public and private activities.[^cite_note-225] The Generalitat uses Catalan in its communications and notifications addressed to the general population, but citizens can also receive information from the Generalitat in Spanish if they so wish.[^cite_note-226] Debates in the Catalan Parliament take place almost exclusively in Catalan and the [Catalan public television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TV3_\(Catalonia\)) broadcasts programs in Catalan.

Due to the rising [immigration](10499603.md)[^cite_note-227] which Spain in general and Catalonia in particular experienced in the first decade of the 21st century, many foreign languages are spoken in various cultural communities in Catalonia, of which [Rif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riff_language)-[Berber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berber_language),[^cite_note-228] [Moroccan Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moroccan_Arabic), [Romanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_language)[^cite_note-229] and [Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu) are the most common ones.[^cite_note-230]

In Catalonia, there is a high social and political consensus on the language policies favoring Catalan, also among Spanish speakers and speakers of other languages.[^cite_note-presencia.cat-231][^cite_note-232][^cite_note-233][^cite_note-234] However, some of these policies have been criticised for trying to promote Catalan by imposing fines on businesses. For example, following the passage of the law on Catalan cinema in March 2010, which established that half of the movies shown in Catalan cinemas had to be in Catalan, a general strike of 75% of the cinemas took place.[^cite_note-235] The Catalan government gave in and dropped the clause that forced 50% of the movies to be dubbed or subtitled in Catalan before the law came to effect.[^cite_note-236] On the other hand, organisations such as [Plataforma per la Llengua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plataforma_per_la_Llengua) reported different violations of the linguistic rights of the Catalan speakers in Catalonia and the other Catalan-speaking territories in Spain, most of them caused by the institutions of the Spanish government in these territories.[^cite_note-237]

The Catalan language policy has been challenged by some political parties in the Catalan Parliament. [Citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizens_-_Party_of_the_Citizenry), a now extra-parliamentary party, is credited with breaking the consensus around language policy in the late 2000 through the 2010s. Nowadays, the [far-right](221220.md) [ultra Spanish nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_nationalism) party [Vox](41928488.md) is the main supporter of a linguistic policy that favours Spanish in Catalonia.[^cite_note-238] The Catalan branch of the [People's Party](404727.md) has a more ambiguous position on the issue: on one hand, it demands a bilingual Catalan–Spanish education and a more balanced language policy that would defend Catalan without favoring it over Spanish,[^cite_note-239] whereas on the other hand, a few local PP politicians have supported in their municipalities measures privileging Catalan over Spanish[^cite_note-240] and it has defended some aspects of the official language policies, sometimes against the positions of its colleagues from other parts of Spain.[^cite_note-241] It must be stated, however, that historically none of these three parties have held significant offices in either regional or local government, never having governed the Generalitat, presided any [Diputació](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provincial_deputation_\(Spain\)) or [Consell Comarcal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consell_comarcal) and as of 2025 having 4 mayors out of 947.[^cite_note-242]

## Culture

### Art and architecture

Main article: [Art of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_of_Catalonia)





Left: [Joan Miró](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joan_Mir%C3%B3). Right: [Antoni Gaudí](31566384.md)

Many important artists are from Catalonia. [Salvador Dalí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador_Dal%C3%AD), [Joan Miró](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joan_Mir%C3%B3) and [Antoni Tàpies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoni_T%C3%A0pies) are some of the most famous Catalan painters. Closely linked with the Catalan pictorial atmosphere, [Pablo Picasso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pablo_Picasso) lived in Barcelona during his youth, training them as an artist and creating the movement of [cubism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cubism). Other important artists include [Claudi Lorenzale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claudi_Lorenzale), for the medieval [Romanticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism) that marked the artistic Renaixença; [Marià Fortuny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mari%C3%A0_Fortuny), for the Romanticism and Catalan Orientalism of the nineteenth century; [Ramon Casas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramon_Casas) or [Santiago Rusiñol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_Rusi%C3%B1ol), main representatives of the pictorial current of Catalan modernism from the end of the nineteenth century to the beginning of the twentieth century; [Josep Maria Sert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josep_Maria_Sert), for early 20th-century [Noucentisme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noucentisme); or [Josep Maria Subirachs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josep_Maria_Subirachs), for expressionist or abstract sculpture and painting of the late twentieth century.





Left: [Museu Nacional d'Art de Catalunya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museu_Nacional_d'Art_de_Catalunya), [Barcelona](4443.md). Right: [Dalí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dal%C3%AD) Museum, [Figueres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Figueres)

The most important painting museums of Catalonia are the [Teatre-Museu Dalí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teatre-Museu_Dal%C3%AD) in Figueres, the [National Art Museum of Catalonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Art_Museum_of_Catalonia) (MNAC), [Picasso Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Picasso_Museum,_Barcelona), [Fundació Antoni Tàpies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fundaci%C3%B3_Antoni_T%C3%A0pies), [Joan Miró Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joan_Mir%C3%B3_Foundation), the [Barcelona Museum of Contemporary Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barcelona_Museum_of_Contemporary_Art) (MACBA), the [Centre of Contemporary Culture of Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_de_Cultura_Contempor%C3%A0nia_de_Barcelona) (CCCB), and the [CaixaForum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CaixaForum_Barcelona).



[Rose window](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rose_window) ([Solsona Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solsona_Cathedral))

In the field of architecture were developed and adapted to Catalonia different artistic styles prevalent in Europe, leaving footprints in many churches, monasteries and cathedrals, of [Romanesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanesque_architecture)[^cite_note-243] (the best examples of which are located in the northern half of the territory) and [Gothic styles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_style). The Gothic developed in Barcelona and its area of influence is known as [Catalan Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalan_Gothic), with some particular characteristics. The church of [Santa Maria del Mar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Maria_del_Mar,_Barcelona) is an example of this kind of style. During the Middle Ages, many fortified castles were built by feudal nobles to mark their powers.

There are some examples of [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_architecture) (such as the Palau de la Generalitat), [Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque) and [Neoclassical architectures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassical_architecture). In the late nineteenth century [Modernism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modernisme) ([Art Nouveau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Nouveau)) appeared as the national art. The world-renowned Catalan architects of this style are [Antoni Gaudí](31566384.md), [Lluís Domènech i Montaner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Llu%C3%ADs_Dom%C3%A8nech_i_Montaner) and [Josep Puig i Cadafalch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josep_Puig_i_Cadafalch). Thanks to the urban expansion of Barcelona during the last decades of the century and the first ones of the next, many buildings of the [Eixample](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eixample) are in the modernist style. In the field of [architectural rationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architectural_rationalism), which turned especially relevant in Catalonia during the Republican era (1931–1939) highlighting [Josep Lluís Sert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josep_Llu%C3%ADs_Sert) and Josep Torres i Clavé, members of the [GATCPAC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GATCPAC) and, in contemporary architecture, [Ricardo Bofill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardo_Bofill) and [Enric Miralles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enric_Miralles).

#### Monuments and World Heritage Sites



The medieval church of [Sant Climent de Taüll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sant_Climent_de_Ta%C3%BCll), located in the foothills of the Pyrenees, in the province of Lleida



[Sagrada Família](59545.md), Barcelona

- [Poblet Monastery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poblet_Monastery), [Poblet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poblet), Tarragona province

  - [Palau de la Música Catalana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palau_de_la_M%C3%BAsica_Catalana), Barcelona

- [Works of Antoni Gaudí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Works_of_Antoni_Gaud%C3%AD):&#x20;

  - [Parc Güell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_Guell), Barcelona
